PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

Last week in opening the subject we got as far as the specialists' clubs. We also dealt with choosing the variety and the hest method t [doing so. I.ItoTITOILE :METHOD OF 1%

A very good way to tell unmistakably if a hen or pullet is about to lay is as follows. On each side of the egg passage is a bone. As a pullet grows to maturity those bones gradually separate until a finger tip can be inserted between Mem ; and when the bird is about to lay the lingers can he passed between these bones. When it Ems ceased to lay they meet once more. Thus one eon tell fairly accurately whether a bird will lay w Ain a week or so. I find this method very helpful especially with liens I am about ti dispose of. Neat week fella!! r üblibh a very interesting para- graph sent in by one of our local readers in larappenhall. Meanwhile beat wishes to you all and many thanks for the letters of appreciation I have to band fr,rn 1.,y

THE ROMANCE OF THE ENGLISH BIBLE.

It was given to a German boy, Johann Gensfleiseh, to stumble upon the art of print- ing, and when, out of .boyish interest, he . ..

carved the letters of his name out of bark

in a very real sense lie was carving out the destiny of nations. The printing press settled the course of the Reformation ; it made possible a general education ; and it gave to the multitude the Bible. It is a significant thing that the first book known to have been printed by Johann Guttenberg (for he took his mother's name in later life) was a Latin Bible.

him to produce better and corrected editions. As to the burning, he gave himself no trouble. who spoke to a defender of the Pope in the glowing phrase" If God spare me I will one day make the boy that drives the plough in England to know more of scripture than the Pope does." There was no room in England for a work- shop such as Tyndale needed. He wanted a press, and liberty to pass through it the printed sheets of the New Testament, but with Henry VIII. as "Defender of the Faith " and Wolsey intriguing for the Pope's chair, Tyndale could hope for nothing. If men were sent to their death for reading Luther's pamphlets, what hope was there that the Bible would be a permitted book? Finding that England offered no place of safety then Tyndale crossed to the Continent to toil in exile at his task. This was in 1524. He was then a man of forty, and he never saw his native land again. First at Hamburg, , then hunted to Cologne and Worms, Tyndale persevered with his task and within four years had managed to secretly print about 15.000 copies of the English New Testament. Smuggled into England by the traders, these passed rapidly into the hands of the people. Thousands of copies were seized and burned at St. Paul's Cross and the Bishops had their agents everywhere to buy and hand over the hated books. TYNDA: es GOOD CUSTOMER.

Of the efforts of the Bishop of London to exterminate the printed Bible a good and true story is told. He sought out Augustine Pakington, a merchant who traded with Antwerp, thinking him to be a likely man to buy up the copies on the other side before they were smuggled across. Pakington was a secret friend of Tyndale and told the Bishop that if he would but furnish money he thought he could easily secure the books.

The Chronicler Halle who tells the story interjects the remark "the Bishop deemed that he hadde God by the toe, whanne in truthe he hadde, as after he thought, the devyl by the fiste." " I will gladly," he said, "give thee what- ever they may cost, for the books are naughty and I intend surely to destroy them all, and to burn them at Paul's Cross." It was not long before Park ington was in the company of Tyndale who was just then sorely in need of funds. "Master Tyndale" he said " I have found thee an excellent purchaser of thy books." "Who is he?" asked the translator. " My Lord of London." "But if the Bishop wants my books it must only he to burn them.- " Well " said Park ington drily "what of that? The Bishop will burn them anyhow, and it is best that you should have money for the enabling you to imprint others instead. 1

' The grand work of Tyndale became the foundation for all who followed, and four out of every five words in our New Testament to-day are just as he placed them. Swift changes were taking place in England during the last days of Tyndale. tinder the sanction and with the help of Canmer and Thomas Cromwell,MylesCoverdale had pre- pared a complete English Bible and this was soon to be followed by the "Great Bible' of 1539. Coverdale made no pretence of original translation. He professed to translate from "Douche and Latin" into English, but, truth to tell, he took the Bible of Tyndale touching it over with his own gentle hand. To those who would like to study his work it is easily accessible in the Prayer Book, for the Psalms as printed there are almost exactly as he prepared them. A good example is Psalm 23.

===== 0002601_19110415_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
destined to become famous as the Geneva Bible, and destined also to become the most popular version. This book for sixty years held its own, and held the th Id for a long time against the Authorised Version of 1611. From its translation of Genesis 3-7 " they sewed fig tree leaves together and made themselves breeches," it has since been familiarly known as the " Breeches " Bible. _ .

But there was one feature of it that made it exceedingly distaseful to the ecclesiastics. It contained numerous marginal notes, all of them of strong Puritan flavour. Because of this, and perhaps because of a recognition of its merits, Archbishop Parker planned a revision of the Great Bible, a work which became known as the Bishops' Bible. The two versions, the " Treacle ' Bible of the Bishops and the " Breeches " Bible of the Puritans, were the two chief rivals in the field when King James I. came from Scotland to rule over us. WHY JAMES DISIAKF.D THE GENEVA BIBLE. Possibly they might have been left to struggle for supremacy for many years had it not been that James found mortal offence in some of the marginal comments of the Puritans' Bible. Two that especially irritated him may be mentioned. In 11. Citron. 15-16 it is written that Asa "removed his mother from being Queen, because she had made an idol in a grove." The Puritans, trained under Calvin, made the grim comment: "Herein he showed that he lacked zeal, for she ought to have died."

In thepassage which tells that the midwives of Egypt disobeyed the King, the margin calmly says " their disobedience to the King was lawful." James, full of Stuart belief in the divine right of Kings, angrily cried "It is false, to disobey a king is not lawful, such traitorous conceits should not go forth among the people." We have not seen a reference to these facts in any of the numerous articles which are appearing upon the Bible of 1611. and we mention them because we believe the King's anger to have been a strong factor in bringing the famous Authorised version into being. It is to be noticed that when giving sanction for the work he expressly stipulated that there should be no marginal notes such as those in the Geneva Bible, and which he described as "very untrue, seditious and savouring too much of dangerous and traitorous conceit." HOW THE ACTHORISED VERSION CAME TO BE MADE. We have only space left to mention the circumstances which brought the Version of 1611 into being. The Puritans had expressed to King James their alarm at the undoing of the work of the Reformation, and he had promised a Conference at Hampton Court to _ meet in Janury, 1604. James loved argu- ment and told a friend afterwards that he had thoroughly enjoyed the encounter. "I have It:Jpt a revel with the Puritans," he said, "and have peppered them soundly." It was whilst discussing the Prayer Book on the second day of the Conference that the desirability of correcting certain errors in the Bishops' Bible came under discussion.and out of this historical conversation the project for the Authorised Version grew. Our historians have given credit to Dr. Reynolds, the Puritan leader, for the suggestion of a new Version. The suggestion may certainly have been his, but we differ from the learned chroniclers in assigning to him the credit. King James bad already been prepared in mind for a scheme of Bible re-translation. In 1601 at the General Assembly of the Church of Scotland the subject had been dis- cussed in the King's presence and he himself had made a speech full of warm commenda- tion of the idea.

At Hampton Court he rose quickly to the proposal and when in the slow course of things the work began King James showed admirable wisdom in choosing 54 scholars of every divergent religious opinion as the men for the task.

Mr. G. K. Chesterton in a fine article has described our Bible as the work of" immortal nobodies." But our brilliant essayist can hardly have considered the facts. The trans- lators built their work upon that of Tyndale, and though they altered scores of words for the worse, eighty out of every hundred words were his. Our Bible is not the work of " immortal nobodies." It is an everlasting memorial to the genius of an "immortal somebody "—to William Tyndale, one of the- greatest, truest Englishmen who ever lived. ' The vers:on was never officially accepted as the Bible of England. From the words on the title page " Appointed to be read in Church "it has been called the Authorised Version, but the word " appointed " meant no more than " allowed." The great book entered into competition with the others and won its triumph as the book of the people no t by authority but by merit.

Notes by Ovalist." Three cheers for the Northern Union! King George has conteuted to become a patron. I wonder it he will ever witness a profetaional Rugby game.

Lancashire is to have, tie final tie for the Northern 'Union Cup to itself, after all. Such a result must have been anticipated when the Yorkshire clubs lost -ground in the earlier stages of the mutnament. Their one representative fought a great fight at Hudders- field on Saturday, but lost. In the League alert • 'Yorkshire are likely to have but one representative in what is called the semi-final tournament, and if one can judge aright it is prettycettain that thin final will ben purely hancathire event a so. The two results, , taken in conjunction, are not flattering to Yorkshire Northern Union football. Imp trtial critics will, bow- ever, acknowledge that the respective merits d the two divisions are accurately represented in these final contests.

So far aa the cup ties are concerned, I do Oct see that Yorkshire folk have much occasion for regret, for this is only the fourth time since the institution of the cup tournament that time has been an exclusively Lancashire final. Swint it and Salford met in 1900; Broughton Rangers and Salford in 1902; mad War- rington and Oldham in 1907. Now Broughton Rangers appear in the final for the 'second time, with opponents in Wigan, who have never previously reached the concluding stage of the tournament. They will fight oat the contest at Salford on the 29th inst.

Wigan were more than a little lucky to appear as finalms after ell. They found Batley in grand cup- lie form, the:clever Wigan Vhacks being completely bottled up. As a team they have not been so well held since Widnes surp ised them in the Lancashire Cup. On Saturday the Wiganers only secured the verdict:by a penalty goal in the last three minutes,and on the day's plav wei e the inferior team. Batley by dint of superb. forward n'ay, were all over the Lancashire cracks at limes, and only want of finish at the critical momer.t caused their efforts to fail. Probably if Wattie !taxies had been able to play the result would have beau diffeient. Anyhow, Wigan have nothing to et ow over in their first uprearance as finalists.

Indication still point to W igen seeming the Cop even though their opponents will not be the team moat people Anticipated. Rochdale Hornets toad. a poor show at Wesate, and were well-beaten by the Ranger., who had twice previously this season bowed the knee to Rothdale. The third meeting found Broughton in a mire resolute mood, and by dint of sterling cup-tie warfare they secured the verdict by a fair margin. It is cur Mus to find the Rangers in the final, for they have been one of the most disappointing teams in the League. When they played Warrington on Febtunry 11th I thought they were one of the poorest sides I had seen for some time. But the luck of the draw, and a fine pack of f.,rwards, have brought them through the Cup Competitivn while many better sides have gone under... I can hardly imagine they will give Wigan much irruble on the 29th.

Warrington indulged in the delights of another victory on Saturday, when the Coventry boys made their first appearance at Wilderspool. The Wire- pullers triumphed by 16 points to 5 after a fairly Interesting game. In the first half there was some particularly lively football, and the home hicks shone at limes. Coventry were also nippy and liable to cause a surprise, but they made poor use of several great oyportunitiee.

The home team were best forward and half-back. Thomas, Shugars, and Cheaters were too vigorous and powerful for the Midlenders and shouldered their way through many a melee. Creevey was the best individual player on the field. His clever swerves though the Coventry defence were a treat to wirnese, but they showed up the r.lownees of the Warrington centres and frequently failed through lace of support. Beyncn played a capital game behind the beaten Coventry puck, and good work was done at times by Cmh and Houghton. Woodburn presented the lecture of a mart player starving ft r opportunities. He got one decent pass through the game, and it was a SOU, ce of astonishment to see the way tire Midlanders ignored their best wing.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

" CUT-TIIROAT COMPETITION."

" Strange that there should such difference he 'twist Tweedledum mud Tvreedledee." One may wrest from its original meaning the well-known and forceful couplet, and apply it to the situation created I_7 that pretty little quarrel between our Gas and !Electricity Oommittees. Such spectacles, while by no means edifying, are rot uncommon. And now, brethren, to resume our oedema discus- sion of the questions to which last week's Town Council debate gave live. The ratepayers at large will spree that there should only be healthy and perfectly good-humoured rivalry between the Cor- poration tracUng departments. It will be observed that I have written "Gas arid" not "Gas versus Electricity," for lad it not been supposed that there was in dos borough "ample 10,1111 and verge enough ' for each, Warrington would nt,t have provided both sources of illumination aud motive power. One Sipleigh, chatting with a lady acquaintance, exclaimed, "kb speaking of electricity, that [mats me think" —and the ether interrupted with " Reilly, Mr. Sap- leigh ! Isn't it remarkable what electricity can do ?" So it is, and yet there are other worlds for it to conquer, if I may so express it. I have jost been dipping . into a comprehensive article from an ' enm geermg correspondent, and, among many other things, the writer remarks that the inttoduction of the metallic filament lamp has brought about an advance in the art of lighting marvellous in ite rapidity and c anplete sumtess. True, the adoption of the new form is an widespread that he is bound to admit that supply testi°t,n have suffered unexpected lows in consequence ; whilst working expose.ses have gone no, the total receipts from current sold have actually gone dawn, but it is reasonable to assume that this extraordinary development will ultimately redound to , the benefit of supplier as well as consumer. What : the metallic filament is destined to accomplish for electricity, the incendescett mantle has certainly done for gas. 'lime was when it was taken for granted that electricity would gradually take the place of the older illuminant for street.bghting purposes ; but most of ilime municipal authorities who hastened to substitute it have lad tmeasion to completely reverse their policy—otherwise they would , asseredly Save risked their reputations for enterprise. Take die Metropolis, for example le Westminster the highways are now lighted midi magnificent gas lamps, and it was recently stated that for equal units of light £B,OOO a year would be saved by using gas in the place of electricity. The lamps in the centre of Regent-street are of a minimum of 3,000 candle- power, and the average light per lamp is said to be nearer 3,500 candle-power. Latady the Holborn Borough Council decided to invite tenders for the lighting of Kingsway, High Holborn and Oxford- street, each lamp in Kingsway to be 1,800 media- power and the rest of 1.500 candle-power. fia, by gas rather than by electricity, is night being turned into day. But there is more beyond all th's. Those excellent friends of ours who are dt ing their utmost for the abatemeat of the smoke nuisance have of Into years taken to strong advocacy of the m are general use of gas for heating and cooking Lecturing not long ago or. "The use of gas in relation to public health, with special reference to smoke abatement," Mr. R. J. Rogers, 1 ghting superintendent of the Birmingham Corporation Gas Department, said that, with regard to the ventilation of a roorn, not only was the use of gas economical, but it was hygienically superior to any other system of artificial illumination. The advocates of electric light contended that electricity was superior to gas on hygienic grounds because electricity did not consume or vitiate the air, while gas did; and also on the ground that it created no heat, and did not damage decorations. Ile qnoted opinions of authorities in support of his argument that the use of gas-lighting of the high candle power had been proved to lie conducive to a healthy atmosphere, and to the well-being of the public who frequented the rooms in which it was used. The superiority of gas from the ventilating point of view had keg been recognised bylmedical authorities Mr. Rogers proceeded to argue that gas light was better for the eyes than electric light; I have proved it so in my own case. Since electricity came to be so extensively applied to other purposes, gas-cooking Iron come into pretty general use. I read in " Chamber's Journal," however, that much attention is now being devoted to the producti Ai of electrically heated cook- ing utensils and devices, while at the a me time the electricity supply compaties—COUßClOUS of the advantages to themselves of a daylight load in the reduction of their working expenses—are reducing their prices for current intended for cooking purposes to a very low rate Nevertheless, the general introduction of electrical cooking appliances in place of those in commoa use to-day would per bibly mean an alarming increaae in the domestic expellees of the average household ; and Warrington need nut' anticipate it. Al in the case of Jack Bunsby, the beaming of these observations lies in the application. The point lam endeavouring to make is that while electricity has a fine field before it and is destined ta expand and develop and bring it.creaairg gain to its promoters, it is not , going to shoulder gas out of the way, nor is it likely to bent fit greatly at the expense of the pa managers. I am freely enaployit g met iphor, ar,d while I am about ill may add that in my view the two should go hand in hand, mutually assisting or , supplementing as occasions arise and as conditions , vary. That where, as in the cove of Warrington, both ate by the local authority held in omit for the rate- payers, there should be nothing capable of description or calling f..r denunciation am "cut-throat competition" I need not labour to prove. PALM SUNDAY.

Sunday last was Palm Sunday. I bad • note con- cerning it a week ago, and in the London correep nd- ence of my Monday's paper I found one to the effect that on the day in question in all the more 'advanced' , churches in London the morning service was preceded by a solemn blessing and distribution of palms. The writer went on—" The special observance of this festival is of ancient origin, and a reference to it ray be found in the writings of St. Cyril of Jerusalem. By the fifth century the carrying of palms in precessions round the churches wan widely practised both in the Greek and Roman Churches As to the ancient ceremony referred to, I may just say that, 'Blesse and sanctifie these brannches of palms. and other trees and flowers,' was the

formula, after which the palms and branches were distributed to the people. Hereabouts, as noted in this colonies week ago, the old church festival is the day ween the willow catkins are in evidence, being commonly called palms—erroneously of course. Really, ate use of p dins and of willows goes further I bark than the entrance to Jerusalem, M ntion is made of it in the Book of Leviticus (chapter 23), where the Israeli.es were directed on the first day of the feast to take "troughs of goodly trees, branche, of palm trees, and the boughs of thick trees, end willows of the brook."

Lever.

The fir.t feeling that will arise in our minds is the deep regret at the severance from Lyrnm cf a family which has always been held in the highest estinna,ion, and, I may add, regarded with sincerest affemion by everyone. The debt which Lymm has owed to the Dewharst Lundy for the past sixty years will be remembered on all sides with true gratitude and appreciation. . . . . .

"-fo those who have been fully acquainted with rill the circumstances, this seven flee will c-nue with no sudden surprise, in fact it was inevitable. THE EXAMINER.

SAIVELDAY. APRIL 15 ICI.

The PRIME MINISTER ;PLOT TO WASTE found it necessary on TIME. Monday to ask for the

suspension of the eleven o'clock rule, which is one of the means of expkiting business. It is conceivable that he might have had a stronger case for doing this ; but it is barely so. The proclaimed policy of the Opposition in the House of Commons is the prevention of progress ; and it is a policy which is having a considerable and exasperating amount of success. The l'arliament Bill is unusually short for a I Government measure ; it contains only seventy-two operative lines. Last week in three full Parliamentary days three and a-half lines got through the Co mmittee stage. The work of the House of Commons stopped in the middle of a sentence. These are the words that the Government managed to pass through Committee in these days . " If a Money Bill having been pos-ed by the House of Commons rind sent up to the House of Lords a, least one month bet,re the end of the

session is not passed by the House of Lords sill,

nut amendment within one month." Absolutely, as will be seen, nothing at all has been decided. The question of whether the House of Lords should have any control over finance was left at the end of last week where it was at the beginning. Yet it is supposed to be a subject concerning which ' party contention does not exist even. Lord LANSDOWNE gave up the claim of the House ,of Lords' to interfere with Money Bills ; 11 what it is that is wanted by the Opposition lin the House of Commons may be matter of opinion. The Opposition certainly does not , want what it professes to be fighting for. It is engaged in nothing more or less than a game of "spoof." Any schoolboy may be set to work out a simple sum in rule of three. If it takes three days to get three and a half lines of print through the Committee stage in the House of Commons, how long a time will be required for the consideration, amend- ment, and approval of seventy lines? The proposed amendments still on the paper are to be counted by hundreds. What is their character? We select an example from one of those that were debated last week. The proposal was that no Budget should go to the House of Lords even for review. This, it seems necessary to state, with some precision, came from the Tory benches. There are scores of proposed amendments which have just as little real intention in them. Their object is open and proclaimed. It is to waste the time of Parliament.

That, however,is not all. There is the baser object of making the proceedings of the House of Commons seem ridiculous. It

might quite naturally be supposed, from what has taken place, that the present Oppo- sition contains much more Parliamentary ability than it has obtained credit for. This impression is dispelled by a peep behind thu scenes and an inspection of the machinery. It then becomes clear that the authors of II the amendments and those by whom they are supported, are merely marionettes. All the motion and impulse comes from outside. There is, it appears, an extra-Parliamentary Committee, which draws up the amendments and supplies draft speeches to the members who take theni in hand. Herein an intima- tion from one of the circulars addressed by this committee to Tory members of Parlia- ment:

When the committee have allocated an amendment to a member, a Copy rf that amend- ment, together with a hi jet note on the point. 1./deed by the amendment, will be sent to the member in whose name it has been put down." The degradation of a party could scarcely, it will be admitted, be carried much lower than this. But if there is a lower depth the Opposition in the House of Commons has reached it. Need we recall the protests that

were made against throwing a shadow over the Coronation by means of the Veto Bill? Surely not. But the present purpose of the Opposition in the House of Commons is to drag the Veto Bill conflict right across the footlights, as it were, and to Jisturb the Coronation proceedings as much as possible. The studied waste of time, let it be borne in mind, has this definite and really despicable purpose, and no other. Further trenchant measures on the part of the PRIME MINISTER to defeat this plot will, we believe, meet with the joyful approval of the country.

The Anglo-japanese. ANOTHER TA RIFF Commercial Treaty ham REFORM BUBBLE pricked the bubble of Hi; RST. retaliation as the

_— Reciprocity Agreement between Canada and the United States pricked the bubble of Imperial Preference. Japan's new tariff was framed on Protec- tionist principles, and our cotton and woollen industries would have been rather hard hit. According to Tariff Reform doctrine, Great Britain was helpless to negotiate for conces- sions in the new tariff, because she was unprovided with the" revolver" of a Protectionist tariff. The Government set to work to get better terms, and the efforts of Sir Edward Grey and Mr. Sydney Buxton have been crowned with a notable success. The chagrin of the "whole- hogging " Tariff Reformer is strikingly manifested iu the extraordinary argument - if it can be called an argument—of the "Morning Post," that Japan must feel a rankling resentment because Great Britain has induced her to lower her tariff wall for , important categories of British goods. The idea that a bargain inspired by friendship and good feeling produces resentment, while concessions extorted by a "big revolver " would be cheerfully made, is one of the most ludicrous figments that the puzzle-headed and topsy-turvy Tariff Reform mind has ever imagined.

The closing meeting Of THE VINDICATION the Free Trade gather- OF FREE TRADE. ings in London was addressed by Mr. Chur- chill. He pointed to three importants events which had vindicated Free Trade policy in a remarkable manner:—(l) The increasing discontent at the high prices of food caused by Protection in Germany, Austria, and the United States; (2) the fact that not the slightest friction or straining at the Imperial bond had arisen betNten this country and Canada in consequence of the commercial departure which Canada is contemplating, and which is a matter for Canada alone to decide; (3) the brilliant commercial treaty negotiated by Sir Edward Grey with Japan, in which for the first time the great free market of this country is recognised, by one of the great contracting Powers of the world, as a greater boon than that offered by the tariffs of other countries. In a close packed sentence Mr. Churchill summed up the creed of Free Traders :—They believe that Free Trade "enshrines certain great economic and moral principles, it preserves the State from corruption, it corrects and prevents monopoly, and it is the handmaid of peace."

It is not the Free IMPORTS AND Traders who selected

exports as the test of our trade's prcsper- ity. It is often a fallacious test. There is a fundamental absurdity in the Protectionist standpoint, from which it is regarded as a misfortune that we should be able to enjoy in profusion all the natural products of the world and all the comforts, conveniences, and improved means of production that the skill and labour of other countries can supply in payment for reciprocal services. But Mr. Chamberlain has set up as the test by which he would have us judge our economic system , the expansion or contraction of exports. Ever since he adopted this test British exports have expanded in marvellous fashion. During the past month they reached the unprecedented figure of £40,863,912, or an increase of 188 per cent., and re-exports were valued at £9,174,666, an increase of 86 per cult. Imports, on the other hand, have increased only o'7 per cent. So much for the Tariff Reform prophecy that our exports under Free Trade, would go on decreasing, and our imports increasing, until we became bankrupt. Of course, the explana- tion of the phneomenon is that we have been largely investing abroad, and the exports are our means of sending out the " money for investment. The interest will come back to us in the form of larger imports in years to come. If it did not, our investments—and exports—would be sheer loss.

EXPORTS.

We are often a•sured A TAX ON Corros ?by Tariff Reformers

that there is no fear that under a system of tariffs raw cotton would be taxed. Many Free Traders have had doubts upon this point, and from time to time have expressed a fear that if we once went in for Tariff Reform, even such a suicidal action as the taxation of our raw cotton supplies might be decided upon. That this fear is not altogether unfounded may be gathered from the following extract from a circular which has been issued by the " United Planters' Association of Southern India" and headed "Imperial Preferential Tariff"

lhat th • Aesociation (.1 affirm In complete adherence to the following creed with Irgard to Imperial Prefert mix! Tariff . We laelieee that attention has not Is en aufficiendy called to the danger to Great Britain and lit.

Empire incurred by relying to such a very great extent on the suppfy of cotton from the United States of America, end that a preferential treat- ment for Brit6h-grown cotton by enconroging adequate product.on within the Empire ir the only way to safeguard the interests of Lancsuhire for all tone A "preferential treatment for British- grown cotton" can only mean a tax on American cotton. We do not need to go deeply into economics or statistics to realise what this would mean to the staple industry ,of Lancashire. Cotton is dear enough already without adding to its price by means of a tariff. It is to be hoped that Lancashire's workers will bear in mind the proposal of the "United Planters' Association" when 'next they listen to the tales of the Tariff ißeformers.

The custom with most COMPULSORY employers is to give the ." CHARACTERS." best " character " they can to everyone leaving their employment voluntarily or by dis- 'missal, but no law governs the custom. No 'employer is obliged to give a "character, and if he does FO he can please himself whether be allows the employe to know what he has written or said. A bill has just been presented to Parliament which has the object of regulating this custom. At the present time there does not seem to be much prospect of the bill becoming law, but if it does nothing eche it will serve to draw popular attention to the subject, and later on some of its principles might be accepted as the basis of a Govern- ment measure. It provides that every employer shall give to every person leaving his employment, upon application, a certificate or character note in writing as to his or her general conduct and competence whilst in his employ- ment. Not only would the employer be so bound, but in addition he would have to give the employee an exact copy of any testi- monial or character note sent to any other employer. The bill further provides that any employer who refused the open character or who wilfully and malicously made false state- ments therein would be liable on summary conviction to a penalty not exceeding £25. It is about thatilast point that any discussion on the bill would be sure to centre. The employer and the employed may naturally have the widest difference of opinion as to the truth or fairness of the "character," and it would be exceedingly difficult in many cases for either side to prove its case,and especially for the servant to prove malice on the part of the employer. Yet it is not to be denied that the principle of the bill is morally sound. Many workpeople, and especially clerks and domestic servants, depend almost entirely upon their " character " for their success in finding fresh employment; they have no other ready proof of their competence or desirability. Because we do not often hear of " characters" being refused or of their being wilfully inaccurate it does not follow that such things are not frequent enough to demand a hard-and-fast law.

T.:l NOTES AND NOTIONS.

At the International Free Trade Congress in 1908, the late Dr. Theodore Barth, then a member of the German Reichstag, declared "that Free Trade is now by far the strongest guarantee of European peace. It is a stronger guarantee than all the triple alliances, and dual alliances, and royal visits, and Hague conferences." • s_

A patriotic London "Gael, pretty well known to members of the Scottish societies in the Metropolis, on one occasion, after having for some time"entertained "a number of English friends to a glowing amount of the beauties of Scotland and its great superiority over England, was asked some- what impatiently, but very pertinently, why he did not stay there. He was not a bit put out. "A verra natural question," he acknow- ledged. "It was like this, ye see. In Scot I land everybody was as clever as mysel'. an' I could mak' naething o't. But here," he added, complacently, "I'm gettin on verra

Many Warrington music lovers will be interested to hear that the Manchester Orpheus Choir, conducted by Mr. W. S. Nesbitt (who is also the conductor of the Warrington Male Voice Choir) will shortly undertake a tour in Germany. According to the present programme the choir will arrive at Frankfort on Saturday, June 10, and stay till the following Thursday. On Sunday it will sing at Wiesbadon, and on Monday at Hamburg. Tuesday will be devoted to sight seeing at Frankfort, and on Wednesday a grand concert will take place at the Saalhaus, the largest hall in the city, the proceeds of 'which will be handed over to the local charities. The Kaiser has expressed his desire to hear the choir.

• • When Mr. Winston Churchill the other day referred to Free Trade as the "Hand- maid of Peace" he was merely voicing the view which has been held by leading states- men ever since Free Trade was established. Richard Cobden wrote: "It has struck me that it would be well to engraft our Free Trade agitation upon the Peace movement. They are one and the same. I believe Free Trade to be the only human means of effect- ing universal and permanent peace. The efforts of the Peace Societies, however laudable, can never be successful so long as the nations maintain their present system of isolation Free Trade, by perfect- ing the intercourse, and securing the dependence of countries upon one another, must inevitably snatch the power from the Governments to plunge their peoples into wars." • • _ _

The question has geen asked repeatedly during the past few days (says the "West- minster Gazette') what would have happened if the result of the scrutiny in the Exeter election petition had been to give Mr. St. Maur or Mr. Duke an equality of votes. It is settled Parliamentary law that if at the termination of a scrutiny the votes are ' equal the election is void. At the Ciren- caster election in the autumn of 1892 Mr. Harry Lawson, who contested the constitu- ency in the Radical interest, was defeated by Colonel Cheaters Master by three votes. A scrutiny resulted in an equality of votes and a void election. By the Ballot Act a return- ing officer may, if on the register of voters and the votes are equal, vote himself and so turn the scale and avoid the necessity of making a double return. If however, he is not on the register, or does not exercise his vote, he must return the election as a tie. When it has been deter- mined as in the case of the Exeter election, that the sitting member was not duly elected, and that some other candidate was duly elected, the Clerk of the Crown is ordered to attend and amend the return by substituting the name of the duly elected candidate for the name of his rival.

Owing to a Machinery break. down this morning, we are compelled to issue the paper hand-folded and uncut, and apologise to our readers for the Inconvenience.

We are glad to note that there is no sug- gestion of careless driving on the part of the taxi-cab chaffeur whose vehicle ran over and killed a little girl in Penketh on Tuesday. But it is a regrettable fact that motorists do frequently pass through this populous village at an excessive speed,and the wonder is that there have not been more fatalities. We think the police ought to pay greater attention to the matter, and report every motorist who exceeds the legal speed limit on this busy and narrow road. • •

Elsewhere we print a cutting from the "Illustrated London sews" of December 7th, 1872, which contains interesting com- Iments on the late Mr. Peter Rylands, M.P. We notice that he had sized-up the landlord class in a manner which will probably not appeal to his present-day descendants. "The notion that this class should be relieved from some of the burdens of local taxation was treated with rude scorn " by the out- spoken member for Warrington.

Sankey Canal Bridges ie,after all, to be only 40 feet wide when re-built. This is a poor contrast to the 80 feet of the new Warring- ton Bridge at the Cheshire end of the town. A deputation from the Corporation has been endeavouring to persuade the County Council to sanction a larger scheme, but has failed. There will be some thankfulness, however,when the 40 foot bridge is provided. Only the other day two young ladies were nearly crushed to death by a four-horse 'bus when crossing the present ridiculously narrow and dangerous structure, and one of them sustained rather severe injury. . . . .

American papers are not famous for their epigrams if they are notorious for their head- lines. The "San Francisco Call," however, recently blossomed forth into a baker's dozen of' notes and comments" which remind one of the petites mooches of a Neapolitan journal. The following are interesting as expressirg the cynicism and humour of the typical American mondain: It costs more to maintain a aloe then a family. Man learn. from every experieumr, except the experience with a woman in it. Nothing come, to ov without effort. Th, best looking cAlars for men are the hardest to button. Some men are born great, some have greatness thrust upon them, and others live in Sc,, Franck., Roosevelt says the Chinese at least •re not two. faced. That's right, for if John had another face he surely would not weer the one he does. The humour is not ours. V. e should be too polite to say that " a man's hair turns grey faster than his wife's because he wears it all the time." La Rochefoucauld would have shivered at such a boa mot. No one but an American could say it, and not offend even his wife. • • _ _

Warrington's Coronation mugs, tenders for which were accepted this week, are guaranteed to be of British manufacture. There has been such a pretty bother about the Wallasey Coronation Committee's order for German-made mugs that the committee in charge of the arrangements in the town has issued the following illuminating state- ment :—" The so-called German mug selected by the Wallasey Coronation Committee is made in Thuringia, in Germany, on behalf of British proprietors, from British china clay, and under the formula of a British patent which has now expired. Had the British patentees been able to secure the necesaary technical skill the article would have been manufactured entirely in England. This particular design of Coronation mug was chosen on account of its artistic and intrinsic superiority." The Committee has definitely decided not to repudiate the contract.

Mrs. Carry Nation, whose death has been reported this week, told an interviewer when she was in this country two years ago that often when she was refreshing herself during her speeches with a glass of water she gave the following toast : Here is the driok that Goi gave to the human family. And in thst gla.s are no sorrows, no woes, no Oars, no blows—nothing Out suggests a widow's moan or an orphan's cry. How csol- ing to the fevered brow, how healthful t the parched holy ! Mny you always drink as ideal drank before there waa any curse ! When told that "you can't get everybody to believe "in the wholesomeness of pure cold water Mrs. Nation remarked, "Can't get them? I don't belong to the ' can't ' crowd. You know my name and my initials, 'Carry A. Nation,' It wasn't for nothing I was given that name."

Lord Onslow has been making a great out- cry about the taxes he has to pay in this country, but he might easily go farther and fare worse. In New York State the tax on large estates left to collateral heirs rises to a maximum of £25 per cent., and even to direct heirs the taxation of largo bequests is very heavy, with the result, say the rich people and their friends, that "securities are being moved from New York, and rich men are acquiring residences elsewhere." In other States the lot of the rich man is still worse, according to the "Philadelphia Record," which shows how the taxation of various States overlaps. Oklahoma is the most extortionate of all the States, for there the taxation really seems to amount to robbery. If, for instance, a resident of New York were to leave to a friend two million dollars in Oklahoma securities, the tax of that State would be 1,951,930d015., leaving only 48,070d015. with which to meet the demand lof the New York tax collector for 418,745d015. So that the bequest of a Oklahoma friend might, indeed, land even a rich man in bank- ruptcy. Oklahoma evidently objects to the exportation of capital. On the whole, we think that our own rich have a better time than their contemporaries in America. • •

Warrington people will be interested to hear that Sir Luke Fildes, R.A., has been commanded to paint a State portrait of the King. The distinguished painter was born in Liverpool in 1844, and had a bent for art in his early days, but IN there was no art school in Liverpool at that time he went to Chester to carry on his studies. His training was continued at the Warrington School of Art and it is a source of pride to Warring- tonians to know that the institution played such an important part in moulding the ..areer of one who has since added lustre to England's reputation in the world of art. Sir Luke Fildes, who was knighted in 1906, came into prominence with his famous ,picture " The Doctor." So many legends are attached to "The Doctor" that is worth while to give the correct version of its creation. The painter was struck by the remarkable earnestness of a doctor at the bedside of a patient," but," he statedr "1 bad

normous difficulty in yainting the actual doctor of the picture, for the one I had in my mind died before I was able to commence the work. His personality had, however, obtained such a hold of me that I at last determined—it was a rash thing to do, at least so it seems to me now—to paint him from memory." This will not be Sir Luke's first experience of Royal sitters, for some years ago, when Queen Alexandra was Princess of Wales, he painted her portrait. "The most patient sitter I ever had" was his description of her Majesty.

• •. There was much ado at Wednesday's meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians about the proposal to build a new dining hall and bring the kitchen accom- modation up-to-date. The scheme involves an expense of £5,600 and the Rev.. A. M. Mitchell, who filled the congenial ro:e of critic, did not hesitate to characterize it as a piece of gross extravagance. There is a great deal in his arguin nit that the present is not an oppor- tune time to extend Workhouse premises,' when several factors are unmistakably tend- ing to a reduction of pauperism, but the scheme had received careful consideration, and commended itself to an overwhelming majority of the Board on the ground that the existing conditions inflict unnecessary dis- comfort upon the inmates.

"Plmt,graphic Punetures."—This was the title Captain St. john Broadbent, of the Damen Photo- graphic Society, gave to his lecture on Tuesday. Railway .Ambolance. Competition.

The eighteenth ambulance crompetition in connection with the Cheshire Lines Pailway, frok place at Liver- pool on Saturday. Nine learns took part, including the Warrington Central team, who secured third place in the recent contest for the Warrington Ambulance Challenge Shield. They were put through very severe fitst-aid tests by Dr. F. E. de Piro, of Barrowford, North Lancashire. The work was car led tut very expeditiously, the men giving practical demonstrations of the utility of the lire-sal knowledge by men engaged in railway work. Possenger, pinto; 4th -Warrington, Central, 172 points. This club, newly formed from the various Wesley Guild Cycling Clubs in the than had its first run as Saturday. Although the tun was to Knutsford the starting place was Victoria Square, Stockton heath, as the captain had planned a new route. Eighteen members from flowery-road, Bold street and Stockton Heath started Stretton-wards. At the Smithy, Antra., they turned on to the old Budworth-road, and found the going excellent, the wind favourable, and of course, no bug-bears, in other words, no motor. The basin if this lane are covered in many parts at present with the leaves of the English Arum, or Cuckoo Pine, and of Dog's Mercury, one of the few public places so near Warringt ut where therm inter- esting plants still grow. Round Arley Perk the wind faced the group and continued FO to Enutsford. At Tabley l'ont OffiPe they turned right instead of goir.g over the hill and left again at the corner of Tabley Park. Tea was partaken of at the Cranford Cafe and the return journey was taken by the main road. The distance of this delightful round is 26 miles front Market Gate and back. • THE CORONATION.

Geiurchi

- REV. F. E. EVANS, VICAR OF AI'PLETON THORN. Of all the rural parishes in the neighbourhood of Warrington, male as more peacefully or pleasantly ' situated than Appleton Thorn—an out of the world village known chiefly to cyclists and pedestrians who pase through it in the summer months on their way to Arley and other popular Cheshire resorts. At the crest of the long and winding road which rives steadily from Lamb Brook, stands the beautiful little church of St. Cross. It is a modern building—having been consecrated in 1886—and is designed an simple but dignified lines. The parishioners take a pride in the servi cee, which are bright and devotional, and there is a regular and loyal congregation. The Rev. F. E. Evans, M.A., came to Appleton Thorn eleven years ago. Since then, although the parish is • very small one, much has been started and kept going, including a parish magatine which pays its way without trouble, and a flouriehing village musical and dramatic society which exercises s beneficial and refining influence on young parishioners, occupies long winter evenings, and provides ercellent enter- , tainmei., for the neighbourhood. A considerable sum of money has been raised in the parish for religions and charitable objects, the chief amount being £230 for • new church organ to replace the imams:tent which had been in the c:inrch since its opening and had become worn out. Toe Vicar had this scheme particularly at heart and, backed up by a united body of workers, he NVSS able to carry it to a most successful fruition. Musically the services at Appleton are much above the average for a email village choir. The Vicar also devotes a great deal of attention to the work of the church school ahi,di was practically rebuilt three years ago. The Rev. F. E. Evans was educated at London University and Exeter College, Orti,rd, where he obtained hie degree in 1886. Before coming to Appleton, he held curaciesat Hollinwood, Lancashire ; All Saints, Portsmouth ; St. Stephen's, Twickenham and Tilehurst, Berke. He is a clove student of Church history and archaeology and has also travelled a great deal. Four years ago he visited India as chaplain to the troops Good Friday.—The Devotion of the Three Hours.

The Devotion of ih--e--ihree Hours formed a prominent feature of the Good Friday observances in many of the Anglican churches in Warrington and district. The service has now obtained a strong bold on Church people of all shades of thought, as it brings home a ith remarkable vividness and simplicity the lessons of the Crucifixion. As to its origin and character we quote the following from as interesting aiticle contributed to the " Manchester Guardian " by Mr. W. E. G. Russell, the famous author of " Collections and Recollections " :—Leaving arid con- , troversies in their proper place, I turn to another form of instruction, simpler, more Biblical, more truly "Teaching" than any Catechism, whether emanating from Rome, or Lambeth, or The Memorial Hall. Many of us have grown up under it, though the days of Catechism lie far back in the past. Year aft, year we have profited by it, all the more richly because we only hear it once a year. Protestants to the backbone, we are not ashamed to confess that meows it to the Jesuits ; and those who look below the surface in religion see in it the strongest reinforcement, from an unexpected quarter, of the Evangelical confession. On Good Friday, 18115, the " Devotion of the Three Hours," which had long been popular on the Con- tinent, was conducted for the first time in the then unknown, now famous, Church of St. Alban, Holborn. The Roman Church and the English Church alike prescribe liturgical services, of great dignity and significance, for the day which oar common Christianity calls Good Friday. But it was char- acteristic of the Jesuits, with their trained insight into the needs of human nature, to provide,in addition to the prescribed rites of the Church, a supplementary form of devotion, essentially popular, simple and human. . . . The appointed services of the Prayer Book are duly rendered in the morning and evening,but the central hours of the day are dedicated to this special use. The church, however richly ornamented at ordinary times, is stripped of all decoration. The alter is have. There is no choir, no procession, none of the pomp and circumstance of worship. As the clock strikes noon,the preacher ascends the pulpit,and briefly introduces his subject. " Flom twelve till three our Lord hung in darkness and agony on the Cross. From twelve till•three we will try xi follow His sufferings, and to think what they mean." The Seven" Words from the Cross " supply the framework of the service. Each is introduced with an appropriate hymn. Each is made the subject of a short, grove, searching address. Then there is a pause for meditation and silent prayer, and then the preacher prays aloud in words with which the con- gregation are familiar. Then another hymn, the next Word " and so till all the Seven are finished. It is now close on three—the "Ninth Hour" of the Jewish computation. Amid a breathless silence, the clock , strikes; slowly the congregation rises from its knves, ' and sings the closing hymn. The blessing is given and tho " Devotion of the Three flours "in ended. It is a remarkable fact but confirmed by universal testimony, that the Three Hours Service never palls or drags. The frequent change of posture, the wise altereaiion of speech and silence, prayer and hymn, prevent any feeling of weariness. Indeed, the moments paws all too soon, and the service is half over before we know that it lieu begun. Emotion must necessarily enter into such a commemoration ; lant it is emotion sedulously entrained, it finds its only vent in the deep music of the Passion Hymns. Never hovel heard " When I survey the wondrous __ .

Cross "and " Rock of Ages" as .g as they are sung in the Service of the Three Hours.

The Holy Week services at the Warrington Pdrish Church have been of a deeply impressive character, a mission having been conducted by the Rev. Eustace Hill, of the Community of the Resurrection, •liilleld. The daily oMces of the Prayer flesh have been Rupp's- mented by a special evangelistic service, a ith address, each evecine, nod large congregations have assembled. The Rev. St. John's Presbyterian Church.

THE FOUNDER OF SUNDAY SCHOOLS.

It is a hundred years ago since Rohrrt Raikes, the founder of the Sunday-school moiement, died, and the Sunday School Union commemorated the event by an interesting ceremony round the statue of Raiker, which was erec'ed by teachers and scholars of Sunday Schools in Great Britain in 188010 commemorate the centenary of Sunday Schools, and stands in the Embankment Gardens, near Charing Cross. The Lord Mayor and Lady Mayoress, and several descendants of Raikes were present. During a brief ceremony Miss Ratko, a grand-niece of the founder of Sunday Schools, placed a laurel wreath ambeeribed for by Sunday-school children at the foot of the rtatue.

===== 0002601_19110415_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, NP.

HOW THE PUBLIC MONEY GOES.

We bea-r--;good deal of talk aicnit the way in which the National Revenue is raised, but, apart from the expenditure upon thit Navy, very little about the way in which ft is spent. In the course of a few weeks, the I Chancellor of the Exchequer will present his Budget to the House of Commons, and he will propose an expenditure for the financial year of probably not less than £180,000,0001 This is a stupendous sum of money to spend annually upon national services, and it behoves the country to keep a keen eye on it in order to see that it gets value for the millions it spends. As has often been pointed out in this column, there is not, under the present Parliamentary system, any effective House of Commons check upon expenditure at all. Some nine- teen days a year are given to the considera- tion of the Estimates, and this is totally inadequate even to make pretence of dis- cussing two-thirds of the Votes. On the last day of Supply, all the Votes which have not been already passed are put to the vote with- out a word of discussion, and in this way probably from eighty to a hundred millions of money will be voted. rhe National Expenditure is divided into three chief groups—the Army, the Navy, and the Civil Service. The purposes for which the seventy five millions to be voted this year for the Army and Navy are, required are fairly well understood, but thel general public is not familiar with the nature of tbe expenditure which is included in the Civil Service Votes. The popular impression is that this expenditure is confined to paying salaries to the members of the Civil Service. It is not quite so bad as that. For the current year the expenditure under the head of "Civil Service' is estimated to amount to £71,865,718, inclusive of the expenditure of the Post Office which is a Profit making concern. This Civil Service expenditure is far more than the total expenditure of the nation a few years ago, and it has risen in the last eight years by over £18,000,000. THE MAIN ITEMS.

This Civil Service expenditure includes all the Government grants for Education, and it also includes the cost of Old Age Pensions. In this year's estimate there is a provision for Education, Science, and Art amounting to £19,141,264. The cost of Old Age Pensions for the current 12 months is estimated to be £12,415,000. These two are the main items of expenditure in the Civil Service Vote, but it will be seen that the two combined do not amount for half the total amount. The other large item of expenditure under the head of "Civil Service" is a sum of £25,077,845 for the Revenue departments, of which over £21,000,000 is for the working of the Post Office, The remainder of this Vote is to meet the cost of collecting the Customs, Excise and Inland Revenue. This leaves, roughly about £14,000,000, which is the cost of running the rest of the State Departments, excluding, of course,the Army and Navy and the cost of the Civil Service branches of these services.

The total cost of the Customs. Excise, and Inland Revenue departments for the present financial year is estimated at just under £4,000,000. The amount of revenue which will be collected by these departments will amount to probably about £180,000,000, so that the cost of the collection of the National IRevenue is under 214 per cent. One-fifth of the expenditure of these departments is accounted for by pensions and superannua- tion allowances, so that the working expenses of the Revenue departments cannot be said to be excessive. The cost of collecting the national revenue will compare very favourably with the cost of the management of any well-conducted business concern ; and it is strikingly economical by comparison, say, with the working expenses of the great insurance companies.

The .E 14,000,000 which is expended upon the non-revenue producing departments of the Civil Service comprises a vast number of items, some of which are very interesting and suggestive. For pensions and retiring allowances to members of the Civil Services, a sum of £2,607,063 is required for the present year. This sum does not include the pensions to members of the fighting forces. The amount of the pensions paid to retired Post Office servants amounts to £856,000 a year. Nearly £3,500,000 is required to meet the cost of the upkeep of the Government buildings, about a quarter of a million of this being for the maintenance of the Royal Parks and Palaces. The upkeep of Osborne coats the nation £12,200 a year, the cost of fuel and lighting and water accounting for f 2,670. Neither Osborne nor the Royal Parks are for the exclusive use of Royalty, much of the expense being for public purposes. The Houses of Parliament cost the nation over £50,000 a year to maintain. The expense of warming, ventilating, and light- ing the building costs £28,000, and the renewal of the furniture runs to an annual expenditure of nearly £4,000. For the present year, a sum of £4,345 is set aside for New Works and Alterations in the Houses of Parliament, and among the items which make up this total is one which reads " Rearranging Robing Room of Parlia- mentary Counsel, £335."

===== 0002601_19110415_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE LORDS COST.

At the time when the House of Lords is figuring so prominently it may be interest- ing to know what the cost of the upkeep of this institution is. The salaries of the various officials connected with the House of Lords amount to £44,059 for the present year. The Serjeant-at-arms who attends upon the Lord Chancellor, gets a salary of £l,OOO a year, and he is also in receipt of a pension as a naval officer. He has a deputy who gets £350, and this person also holds two other offices for which he draws another £250. The Depart- ment of the Gentleman Usher of the Black Rod costs the nation £4,297 a year. There is' a Black Rod himself, who has £l,OOO a year,' and he has also a naval pension of £B9O. He has an assistant who is called the Yeoman Usher of the Black Rod, and he gets .C3OO for the post, and £2OO as Secretary to Lord Great Chamberlain. The fifteen doorkeepers of the House of Lords draw £2,997 a year. The salaries of the House of Commons officials run away with a sum of £65,000 a year. . . .

w Another department of the State which necessarily costs a lot of money to maintain is the Stationery and Printing office. The nation does not do its own printing, but the amount of work which it has to give out would certainly keep a very large State prin- ting establishment going. The cost of stationery and printing, for the State depart- ments for the current year, is estimated to amount to just a million pounds. The Houses of Parliament run up a printing bill of about .C 93,000 a year. The cost of printing the daily reports of the proceedings of the House of Cammons is about £1 4,000 for the session. The reporting is now done by a staff under

thb control of the Speaker, and by first post on the following morning a full report of the preceding days proceedings up to 11 p.m. is sent to each member. MISCELLANEOUS EXPENDITURE. Some very exaggerated ideas are abroad about the cost of Royal Commissions. The impression is very general that the members of such Commissions are paid huge fees for their services. Such is not the case, mem- bers receiving no remuneration whatever. There are some dozen Royal Commissions sitting at present, and the total estimated cost for the year is £33,000. A great pert of that will be spent in paying the expenses of witnesses who are summoned to give evidence.ln the estimates for the present year [there are a number of extraordinary !expenses. The following two items come together in the printed statement, "Duke of Connaught's Visit to South Africa £40,000 Expenses under the Unemployed Workmen Act £100,000." There is also a special item this year of £185,000 as "expenses on account of the Coronation of His Majesty." This is in addition to a sum of about £90,000 which is included in the Army Vote. To go through the hundreds of pages in which all the particulars of the Civil Service expenditure are set out impresses one with a strong desire to see some much more effective House of Commons supervision of the details of this expenditure. There is a ' Public Accounts Committee which examines the expenditure after it has been spent, what is wanted is a Committee which will have authority to see that proposed expenditure is necessary. If this were done, there is no [ doubt about it that millions a year might be [ saved.

SCIIOOI..

The 105th anniversary of the opening of the St. Helens Congregational Sonday school was celebrated on Monday, by en interesting series of services, at which the prencher was the Rev. J. Ciwilyin Jones, pastor. In tie afternoon a united service for the schools and tnen's brotherhood isms bold. The children ren- dered untie! pleasing 000icnl ten Mr. Andrew Thompson, superintendent, presided. Rallies, the founder of Sunday reboots, wan born in Gloucester in 1133, and died in 141 I, and he was led to begin his Sonday school on account of the Ignorance and wickednue of the people his native town. There was a parallel between that and the founding of their Sunday school, for it was the awful death of s most profuse young man, who was struck dead by lightning, that led to the Rev. Isaac Sharp founding their Sunday school in 106. The Rev. J. Gwilym Jonea preached specially to the Sunday schools, and said that God had given all the young people flu gifts—physical strength, intellect, the affections, the will, and the conscience. They should not neglect their games, but should develop their physical powers to that they might bs fitted for this battle of life. They cereal, responsible for the right employment of the mind. Collections were taken for the carrying on of the school work, Vid being the necessary amount.

1101 IE RULE FINANCE.

It will probably be recalled that we Sated some lime ago that the Government were. in addition to the actuaital investigation of Irish Finlnce, about to appoint a committee to outsider the Financial Aspects of Home Rule. The formation of that committee bas now been officially snnounced and our reason for referring to it here is that many of the papers seem to have entirely misunderstood the character and objects of the committee. The personnel of the coin. mittee has been the subject of considerable comment It is,we would point out, merely an advisoty body. It will take no evidence in public nor will it issue any report, its only object being to assist the Govern- ment on the various points arising in or nnection with the finances of Home Rn'e It will, probably, only take such evidence as it need, for the carrying Gut of Its task and that evidence in the nature of things sill be sought from specialist. in their particular branches of finance. Mr. Birrell's answer in the House recently makes this clear. The Irish Party are strisfied with the constitution of the committee and regard Ito, well equipped for the highly important work its memlers have undertaken. PROGRESS IN THE FIGHT..

A NEW HISTORY OF IRELAND.

Car'vle's idea of • university at hoot in a well select d library is an idea ssuredly capsble of realisation in this age of excellent and cheap reprints of masterpieces of literature. With the • Everyman " series we imegined that the lest ward had been ea in the issue of valuable books at a price within teach of the poorest artisan, but when we looked over the volumes in the " Horne University Library " we found this progre-sive art of publication carried even further. Espvially do we desire to commend to al Irishmen and Englishmen also a most suggestive and valuable little book in this series, " Irish Nstionality," a brief and powerful eudy by Mrs. J R. Green. It is a book of rare quslity. In three. hours you are conducted by a guide whose historic scholarship none disputes through the long and chequered story of the Irish people. There i 3 no tedious detail, rather is the history suggested than directly told in this shilling book, yet with the greatest cleverness Mrs. Green shews the main forces and the bold lines of Irelancre story in a form which even the busiest reader , will be able to follow. The long life if the Irish civilisation, the unbroken splendonr of its learning, and the fine balance of its political and nodal custom, the dire struggle agetnst the attempts of a foreign mind to wreck it, all these are shown with so fair-minded and scholarly a . . . .

presentation, that any min, however advanced in his study or natii.nv aol their stories, cannot but leave ths b.3_,lv with a fre,b an I vivid outline in his mind of the great underlying forces of Irelin,l's unparalleled past. We recommend everyone who dedrest get at the heirt of Ireland's detriald to-day to buy this splendid abillingsworl awl carefully, with nnt,bink sod pencil, to read every line of it.

ANIMALS WITH HUMAN WAYS.

An old circus proprietor thus discoursed concerning the human traits shown by wild animals. _

Nearly all animals, he said, are vain, and all are of a jealous disposition. Even the best-tempered creatures are annoyed at the attention paid to other animals. I have known elephants to grow sulky as the result of watching people displaying an interest in other creatures to the neglect of the great animals in the elephant house. Tigers are very fond of being taken in parade through the streets, and get impatient when preparations are being made for this purpose. When the procession starts they show evory sign of pleasure, for they love the crowds, the music, and the excitement. A noticeable trait about wild animals is their love of children. They seem instinc- tively to recognise the helplessness and innocence of childhood, and it is a very rare thing to hear of them attacking young people. The story of the savage elephant which was quieted when the keeper's wife, whom it was about to attack, threw her baby in front of it, is a case in point. Elephants, in fact, by reason of their intelligence, show affection, grief, remorse, and other emotions in a marked degree. A female elephant which had been badly treated by her keeper, at dusk or.e evening attacked another keeper, mistaking him for the offender. Fortunately, she discovered her mistake, and, turning the unconscious , man over with her trunk, she tried her best to revive him. When at last he came to him- self she was overwhelmed with delight, which she showed by every possible means in her power. But she had not forgotten her grudge against the other attendant, for within a few days she caught him unawares and nearly killed him.

I Another elephant died of a broken heart. I She was the doting mother of a baby I elephant, on which she lavished all her cares I and affection.

One day the little elephant Vra9 removed to another part of the menagerie, and the mother, taking it for grar ted that the separa- tion was permanent, became greatly depressed. Hearing her young one call to her from a distance, the old elephant trumpeted loudly in response, and then fell dead.

THIS WEEK'S STORY.

HOW WILLIE SCORED.

" Pa," said Willie, one evening, holding up a seed, " if I plant this seed will it grow into an apple tree ?" "Of course it will," replied his father. "You see, my son, the apple is just a covering for the seed and—" "And will it grow into a big apple-tree, papa r "Of course." returned his parent somewhat snappishly. ' Well, it's very strange, pa, for, you see, this is the seed of a pear."
